## GRB-001 · L1 · easy · Number series · foundation

What comes next in the series?

3, 7, 15, 31, 63, 127, ?

- **A.** 257  _(error: added 3 instead of 1)_
- **B.** 255 ✅
- **C.** 254  _(error: doubled without adding 1)_
- **D.** 191  _(error: repeated the last difference)_

**Working**

1. Rule: Each term = previous × 2 + 1.
2. Terms: 3, 7, 15, 31, 63, 127 → next = 255.

**Formula:** Each term = previous × 2 + 1  
**Trap:** Differences 4, 8, 16, 32 also double — both views give the same next term.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## GRB-002 · L1 · easy · Number series · foundation

What comes next in the series?

5, 6, 10, 19, 35, 60, ?

- **A.** 80  _(error: took differences as multiples of 4)_
- **B.** 76  _(error: repeated the last difference 4²)_
- **C.** 97  _(error: arithmetic slip (+1))_
- **D.** 96 ✅

**Working**

1. Rule: Differences are 1², 2², 3², 4², 5².
2. Terms: 5, 6, 10, 19, 35, 60 → next = 96.

**Formula:** Differences are 1², 2², 3², 4², 5²  
**Trap:** Look at the differences before guessing a multiplier.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## GRB-003 · L1 · easy · Number series · foundation

- **A.** 42 ✅
- **B.** 40  _(error: added 10 again)_
- **C.** 36  _(error: took 6² instead of 6 × 7)_
- **D.** 44  _(error: added 14)_

**Working**

1. Rule: Term n = n × (n + 1).
2. Terms: 2, 6, 12, 20, 30 → next = 42.

**Formula:** Term n = n × (n + 1)  
**Trap:** Differences 4, 6, 8, 10 rise by 2; next difference is 12.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## GRB-015 · L3 · hard · Number series · officer

Find the missing term.

2, 12, 36, 80, ?, 252

- **A.** 125  _(error: took 5³)_
- **B.** 150 ✅
- **C.** 144  _(error: took 12²)_
- **D.** 160  _(error: took differences as +10, +24, +44 … +80)_

**Working**

1. Term n = n² × (n + 1): 1×2, 4×3, 9×4, 16×5.
2. 5th term = 25 × 6 = 150; check 36 × 7 = 252.

**Formula:** n²(n+1)  
**Trap:** Always verify the candidate against the term after the gap.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## GRB-061 · L2 · medium · Letter and digit pair counting · foundation

How many pairs of letters are there in the word 'MONITOR' each of which has as many letters between them in the word as in the English alphabet? (Count a pair once whether the letters run forward or backward.)

- **A.** 1  _(error: counted only forward (alphabetical-order) pairs)_
- **B.** 3 ✅
- **C.** 2  _(error: arithmetic slip (−1))_
- **D.** 4  _(error: counted backward pairs twice)_

**Working**

1. Compare every pair (i, j): letters between them in the word = j − i − 1; in the alphabet = |difference in positions| − 1.
2. Qualifying pairs: ON, NR, TR.
3. Total = 3.

**Formula:** |pos(x) − pos(y)| = gap in word  
**Trap:** Adjacent letters of the alphabet sitting next to each other (like ON) also count.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## GRB-064 · L2 · medium · Letter and digit pair counting · foundation

How many pairs of digits are there in the number 5728391 each of which has as many digits between them in the number as when the digits are arranged in ascending order (0–9 number line)? (Count forward and backward pairs once each.)

- **A.** 2 ✅
- **B.** 3  _(error: double-counted)_
- **C.** 1  _(error: counted only ascending pairs)_
- **D.** 4  _(error: arithmetic slip (+2))_

**Working**

1. For digits a (position i) and b (position j): need |a − b| = j − i.
2. Qualifying pairs: 58, 31.
3. Total = 2.

**Formula:** |a − b| = gap in number  
**Trap:** Descending adjacent digits (e.g. 8 then 7 next to each other) qualify too.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

- **A.** -1  _(error: arithmetic slip (−1))_
- **B.** 2  _(error: ignored the 'preceded by a digit' condition)_
- **C.** 1  _(error: ignored the 'followed by a consonant' condition)_
- **D.** 0 ✅

**Working**

1. Scan each symbol and check its left and right neighbours.
2. Count = 0.

**Formula:** Neighbour checks / position arithmetic  
**Trap:** Read left and right carefully; the end element has only one neighbour.

## GRB-067 · L2 · medium · Letter and digit pair counting · foundation

Study the arrangement:

F & 5 7 6 7 9 @ * C O 5 G U M ★ H # I 7

Which element is 5th to the left of the 9th element from the right end?

- **A.** @  _(error: counted the start element as one step)_
- **B.** 9 ✅
- **C.** 7  _(error: counted from the left end)_
- **D.** H  _(error: moved right instead of left)_

**Working**

1. 9th from right = position 12 from left (5).
2. 5 to its left = position 7 from left = 9.

**Formula:** Neighbour checks / position arithmetic  
**Trap:** Read left and right carefully; the end element has only one neighbour.

- **B.** 4  _(error: arithmetic slip (+1))_
- **C.** 2  _(error: arithmetic slip (−1))_
- **D.** 5  _(error: counted all vowels)_

**Working**

1. Check the left neighbour of each consonant.
2. Count = 3.

**Formula:** Neighbour checks / position arithmetic  
**Trap:** Mark positions from both ends before moving left/right.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## GRB-087 · L3 · hard · Number and letter analogy · officer

Select the pair in which the numbers are related in the same way as 6 : 222 and 2 : 10.

- **A.** 8 : 576  _(error: follows n²(n + 1))_
- **B.** 5 : 126  _(error: follows n³ + 1)_
- **C.** 4 : 60  _(error: follows n³ − n)_
- **D.** 7 : 350 ✅

**Working**

1. 6³ + 6 = 222 and 2³ + 2 = 10 → rule n³ + n.
2. 7³ + 7 = 350 ✓; 5³ + 5 = 130 ≠ 126; 4³ + 4 = 68 ≠ 60; 8³ + 8 = 520 ≠ 576.

**Formula:** n³ + n  
**Trap:** Distractors follow near-miss rules (n³ + 1, n³ − n).

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

- **D.** 60  _(error: multiplied positions)_

**Working**

1. DOG: 4 + 15 + 7 = 26; FAN: 6 + 1 + 14 = 21 → sum of positions.
2. CAT: 3 + 1 + 20 = 24.

**Formula:** Sum of alphabet positions

- **C.** Paternal uncle  _(error: gender of the person reversed)_
- **D.** Maternal aunt  _(error: wrong side of the family (paternal/maternal swapped))_

**Working**

1. Placing members: A(M), B(M), C(M), D(F), E(F), F(F) — the clues admit exactly one tree (checked by enumeration).
2. D → C: paternal aunt.

**Formula:** Fix couples and generations first, then read the relation  
**Trap:** Paternal vs maternal depends on which parent links the two.

| Letter | C | E | F | I | K | M | O | P | R | S | T | U |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Code | 1 | 9 | 4 | 6 | & | # | © | % | 2 | $ | 8 | * |

Conditions (apply only the first condition that holds, in the order given; if none holds, code each letter directly):
(i) If both the first and the last letters are vowels, both are coded as ★.
(ii) If the first letter is a consonant and the last letter is a vowel, their codes are interchanged.
(iii) If the second and the second-last letters are both vowels, their codes are interchanged.
(iv) If both the first and the last letters are consonants, the first is coded as © and the last as δ.

What is the code for 'FIPRT'?

- **A.** 46%28  _(error: applied no condition)_
- **B.** 86%24  _(error: applied condition (ii) wrongly)_
- **C.** ©6%2δ ✅
- **D.** ★6%2★  _(error: applied condition (i) wrongly)_

**Working**

1. Direct codes: 4 6 % 2 8.
2. 'FIPRT': first F (consonant), last T (consonant) → condition (iv) applies.
3. Code = ©6%2δ.

**Formula:** Check conditions in order; apply only the first that holds  
**Trap:** Test the conditions strictly in the listed order — a later condition may also fit.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## GRB-216 · L3 · hard · Letter-to-symbol coding · officer

In a certain code, each letter is replaced by a symbol, but the symbols are NOT written in the same order as the letters.

- 'PESR' is coded as '%$@©'
- 'TRS' is coded as '%#@'
- 'RNS' is coded as '@%&'
- 'NPTE' is coded as '#&$©'

Which symbol stands for the letter N?

- **A.** @  _(error: a symbol shared with a group containing the letter)_
- **B.** & ✅
- **C.** $  _(error: a symbol shared with a group containing the letter)_
- **D.** %  _(error: a symbol shared with a group containing the letter)_

**Working**

1. Letter N appears in: RNS, NPTE; it is absent from: PESR, TRS.
2. The only symbol with exactly the same pattern of appearances is &.
3. Exhaustive check of all letter→symbol assignments confirms N = &.

**Formula:** Match each letter to the symbol with the same appearance pattern  
**Trap:** Intersect the codes of the groups that contain the letter and remove symbols from groups that do not.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## GRB-271 · L1 · easy · Statement and assumption · foundation

An assumption is something supposed or taken for granted — unstated — on which the statement depends. Decide which of the assumptions is/are implicit in the statement.

Statement: The municipal corporation has advised residents to boil drinking water for the next two weeks.

I. Boiling makes the water safe to drink.
II. Residents will ignore the advice.

- **A.** Only assumption II is implicit  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Both I and II are implicit  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Only assumption I is implicit ✅
- **D.** Neither I nor II is implicit  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: The advice makes sense only if boiling removes the danger — implicit.
2. II: Nobody gives advice expecting it to be ignored — not implicit.
3. Hence: Only assumption I is implicit.

**Formula:** An assumption is something supposed or taken for granted — unstated — on which the statement depends  
**Trap:** An assumption must support the statement, not work against it.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## GRB-272 · L1 · easy · Statement and assumption · foundation

An assumption is something supposed or taken for granted — unstated — on which the statement depends. Decide which of the assumptions is/are implicit in the statement.

Statement: Advertisement of a coaching institute — "Join our weekend batch and prepare for bank examinations without leaving your job."

I. The institute's weekday classes are of poor quality.
II. Some working people want to prepare for bank examinations.

- **A.** Both I and II are implicit  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Neither I nor II is implicit  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Only assumption I is implicit  _(error: accepted only I — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Only assumption II is implicit ✅

**Working**

1. I: Nothing about weekday quality is implied — not implicit.
2. II: The weekend batch is aimed at job-holders who want to prepare — implicit.
3. Hence: Only assumption II is implicit.

**Formula:** An assumption is something supposed or taken for granted — unstated — on which the statement depends  
**Trap:** Do not read criticism of other offerings into an advertisement.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## GRB-273 · L2 · medium · Statement and assumption · foundation

An assumption is something supposed or taken for granted — unstated — on which the statement depends. Decide which of the assumptions is/are implicit in the statement.

Statement: Notice in an office — "Please use the stairs; the lift is under maintenance until 5 p.m."

I. Staff are able to use the stairs.
II. The lift is expected to work after 5 p.m.

- **A.** Neither I nor II is implicit  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Only assumption I is implicit  _(error: accepted only I — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Only assumption II is implicit  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Both I and II are implicit ✅

**Working**

1. I: Asking people to use the stairs presumes they can — implicit.
2. II: 'Until 5 p.m.' presumes the work will be over by then — implicit.
3. Hence: Both I and II are implicit.

**Formula:** An assumption is something supposed or taken for granted — unstated — on which the statement depends  
**Trap:** Time limits in a notice carry an assumption about when the situation ends.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## GRB-274 · L2 · medium · Statement and assumption · foundation

An assumption is something supposed or taken for granted — unstated — on which the statement depends. Decide which of the assumptions is/are implicit in the statement.

Statement: The railways have introduced a mobile app for booking unreserved tickets in order to shorten queues at booking counters.

I. Many passengers have access to smartphones.
II. Long queues at booking counters are a problem.

- **A.** Only assumption II is implicit  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Both I and II are implicit ✅
- **C.** Neither I nor II is implicit  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Only assumption I is implicit  _(error: accepted only I — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: An app can cut queues only if many passengers can use it — implicit.
2. II: The stated purpose presumes queues are a problem — implicit.
3. Hence: Both I and II are implicit.

**Formula:** An assumption is something supposed or taken for granted — unstated — on which the statement depends  
**Trap:** Both the means and the purpose of a decision can carry assumptions.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## GRB-275 · L2 · medium · Statement and assumption · foundation

An assumption is something supposed or taken for granted — unstated — on which the statement depends. Decide which of the assumptions is/are implicit in the statement.

Statement: Rahul told his friend, "Buy this laptop; it is the lightest one in its price range."

I. The friend will buy only the lightest laptop available in the market.
II. Heavier laptops are always of poor quality.

- **A.** Both I and II are implicit  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Only assumption I is implicit  _(error: accepted only I — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Only assumption II is implicit  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Neither I nor II is implicit ✅

**Working**

1. I: The advice does not presume the friend buys only the lightest laptop in the whole market — too extreme.
2. II: Nothing is said about the quality of heavier laptops — 'always' makes it extreme.
3. Hence: Neither I nor II is implicit.

**Formula:** An assumption is something supposed or taken for granted — unstated — on which the statement depends  
**Trap:** Words like 'only' and 'always' usually make an assumption too strong.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## GRB-276 · L2 · medium · Statement and assumption · officer

An assumption is something supposed or taken for granted — unstated — on which the statement depends. Decide which of the assumptions is/are implicit in the statement.

Statement: The government has decided to set up fast-track courts to dispose of pending cases of crimes against women within six months.

I. Fast-track courts can decide cases faster than regular courts.
II. Such crimes will stop as soon as the courts are set up.

- **A.** Both I and II are implicit  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Only assumption II is implicit  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Only assumption I is implicit ✅
- **D.** Neither I nor II is implicit  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: The six-month target rests on fast-track courts being quicker — implicit.
2. II: The decision is about disposal of pending cases, not an immediate end to crime — not implicit.
3. Hence: Only assumption I is implicit.

**Formula:** An assumption is something supposed or taken for granted — unstated — on which the statement depends  
**Trap:** Separate the stated objective (speedy disposal) from a hoped-for side effect.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## GRB-277 · L2 · medium · Statement and assumption · officer

An assumption is something supposed or taken for granted — unstated — on which the statement depends. Decide which of the assumptions is/are implicit in the statement.

Statement: A bank has announced that customers who link their accounts to its new app will earn reward points on every digital payment.

I. Reward points may encourage customers to make digital payments.
II. Customers who do not use the app will close their accounts.

- **A.** Only assumption II is implicit  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Neither I nor II is implicit  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Both I and II are implicit  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Only assumption I is implicit ✅

**Working**

1. I: An incentive is offered only if it is expected to influence behaviour — implicit.
2. II: No such consequence is presumed — not implicit.
3. Hence: Only assumption I is implicit.

**Formula:** An assumption is something supposed or taken for granted — unstated — on which the statement depends  
**Trap:** An incentive scheme assumes the incentive works, not that non-users will leave.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## GRB-278 · L3 · hard · Statement and assumption · officer

An assumption is something supposed or taken for granted — unstated — on which the statement depends. Decide which of the assumptions is/are implicit in the statement.

Statement: "Instead of building a new flyover, the city should synchronise its traffic signals," said the transport consultant.

I. Synchronising the signals can ease congestion to a meaningful extent.
II. A new flyover can never reduce congestion.

- **A.** Both I and II are implicit  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Only assumption II is implicit  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Neither I nor II is implicit  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Only assumption I is implicit ✅

**Working**

1. I: Recommending signal synchronisation presumes it will help — implicit.
2. II: Preferring one option does not require believing the other can never work — 'never' is too strong.
3. Hence: Only assumption I is implicit.

**Formula:** An assumption is something supposed or taken for granted — unstated — on which the statement depends  
**Trap:** A preference is not a claim that the alternative is useless.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## GRB-279 · L3 · hard · Statement and assumption · officer

An assumption is something supposed or taken for granted — unstated — on which the statement depends. Decide which of the assumptions is/are implicit in the statement.

Statement: The company has asked all employees to complete the cyber-security module by Friday; e-mail access will be suspended for those who do not.

I. Employees are able to complete the module by Friday.
II. The threat of suspension will encourage employees to complete the module.

- **A.** Only assumption II is implicit  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Neither I nor II is implicit  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Both I and II are implicit ✅
- **D.** Only assumption I is implicit  _(error: accepted only I — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: A deadline is set on the basis that it can be met — implicit.
2. II: The penalty is attached to ensure compliance — implicit.
3. Hence: Both I and II are implicit.

**Formula:** An assumption is something supposed or taken for granted — unstated — on which the statement depends  
**Trap:** A penalty clause carries the assumption that it will deter.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## GRB-280 · L2 · medium · Statement and assumption · officer

An assumption is something supposed or taken for granted — unstated — on which the statement depends. Decide which of the assumptions is/are implicit in the statement.

Statement: District libraries will remain open till 10 p.m. during the examination season.

I. Some students may wish to study in libraries in the evening during examinations.
II. Students do not study at home.

- **A.** Neither I nor II is implicit  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Only assumption I is implicit ✅
- **C.** Both I and II are implicit  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Only assumption II is implicit  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: Extended hours in the examination season presume evening demand from students — implicit.
2. II: Late hours do not presume that nobody studies at home — not implicit.
3. Hence: Only assumption I is implicit.

**Formula:** An assumption is something supposed or taken for granted — unstated — on which the statement depends  
**Trap:** Reject assumptions that turn 'some' into 'all'.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## GRB-281 · L3 · hard · Statement and assumption · officer

An assumption is something supposed or taken for granted — unstated — on which the statement depends. Decide which of the assumptions is/are implicit in the statement.

Statement: The weather department has forecast heavy rain in the coastal districts, and fishermen have been advised not to go out to sea for three days.

I. The forecast is reasonably reliable.
II. Going out to sea during heavy rain is risky.

- **A.** Neither I nor II is implicit  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Both I and II are implicit ✅
- **C.** Only assumption II is implicit  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Only assumption I is implicit  _(error: accepted only I — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: Advice is issued on the basis of a forecast considered dependable — implicit.
2. II: The advice exists because the sea is dangerous in such weather — implicit.
3. Hence: Both I and II are implicit.

**Formula:** An assumption is something supposed or taken for granted — unstated — on which the statement depends  
**Trap:** An advisory assumes both the reliability of its basis and the risk it guards against.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## GRB-282 · L3 · hard · Statement and assumption · officer

An assumption is something supposed or taken for granted — unstated — on which the statement depends. Decide which of the assumptions is/are implicit in the statement.

Statement: To curb absenteeism, the school will send an SMS to parents whenever a student is absent.

I. Most parents have access to a mobile phone.
II. In some cases parents are unaware that their children are absent.

- **A.** Neither I nor II is implicit  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Only assumption II is implicit  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Both I and II are implicit ✅
- **D.** Only assumption I is implicit  _(error: accepted only I — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: An SMS alert works only if parents can receive it — implicit.
2. II: Informing parents helps only if some did not already know — implicit.
3. Hence: Both I and II are implicit.

**Formula:** An assumption is something supposed or taken for granted — unstated — on which the statement depends  
**Trap:** Check both the channel and the purpose of a measure.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## GRB-283 · L3 · hard · Statement and assumption · officer

An assumption is something supposed or taken for granted — unstated — on which the statement depends. Decide which of the assumptions is/are implicit in the statement.

Statement: The state has made helmets compulsory for pillion riders as well.

I. Pillion riders are more careless than riders.
II. Riders who wear helmets never meet with accidents.

- **A.** Only assumption II is implicit  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Both I and II are implicit  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Neither I nor II is implicit ✅
- **D.** Only assumption I is implicit  _(error: accepted only I — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: The rule does not compare carefulness — not implicit.
2. II: Helmets reduce injury, not accidents; 'never' is false and not presumed — not implicit.
3. Hence: Neither I nor II is implicit.

**Formula:** An assumption is something supposed or taken for granted — unstated — on which the statement depends  
**Trap:** The implicit assumption would be that pillion riders also risk head injury — neither option says that.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## GRB-284 · L3 · hard · Statement and assumption · officer

An assumption is something supposed or taken for granted — unstated — on which the statement depends. Decide which of the assumptions is/are implicit in the statement.

Statement: Job advertisement — "Only candidates who have scored at least 60% marks in graduation are eligible to apply."

I. Candidates who scored less than 60% cannot perform the job at all.
II. Enough candidates with 60% or more are expected to apply.

- **A.** Both I and II are implicit  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Only assumption II is implicit ✅
- **C.** Neither I nor II is implicit  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Only assumption I is implicit  _(error: accepted only I — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: A cut-off is a screening tool; it does not presume others are totally incapable — not implicit.
2. II: An employer sets a cut-off expecting an adequate pool above it — implicit.
3. Hence: Only assumption II is implicit.

**Formula:** An assumption is something supposed or taken for granted — unstated — on which the statement depends  
**Trap:** Eligibility cut-offs assume a sufficient applicant pool, not incompetence below the line.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## GRB-285 · L3 · hard · Statement and assumption · officer

An assumption is something supposed or taken for granted — unstated — on which the statement depends. Decide which of the assumptions is/are implicit in the statement.

Statement: Advertisement — "Our new detergent removes stains even in cold water — save on your electricity bill!"

I. Some people heat water to wash clothes.
II. Stain removal matters to consumers.

- **A.** Only assumption II is implicit  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Both I and II are implicit ✅
- **C.** Only assumption I is implicit  _(error: accepted only I — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Neither I nor II is implicit  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: The electricity-saving claim makes sense only if some people heat washing water — implicit.
2. II: The product is sold on its stain-removing ability — implicit.
3. Hence: Both I and II are implicit.

**Formula:** An assumption is something supposed or taken for granted — unstated — on which the statement depends  
**Trap:** An advertisement assumes that its selling points matter to buyers.

## GRB-286 · L1 · easy · Course of action · foundation

A course of action is a practicable and feasible step which, if taken, follows logically from the statement and helps solve, reduce or improve the problem. Assume everything in the statement is true.

Statement: Many students in a village school fail in mathematics every year.

I. Remedial classes in mathematics should be arranged for weak students.
II. Mathematics should be removed from the school syllabus.

- **A.** Both I and II follow  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Only I follows ✅
- **C.** Neither I nor II follows  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Only II follows  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: Remedial classes address the cause directly — follows.
2. II: Removing a core subject is impractical and evades the problem — does not follow.
3. Hence: Only I follows.

**Formula:** A course of action is a practicable and feasible step which, if taken, follows logically from the statement and helps solve, reduce or improve the problem  
**Trap:** A course of action must solve the problem, not abolish the activity.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## GRB-287 · L1 · easy · Course of action · foundation

A course of action is a practicable and feasible step which, if taken, follows logically from the statement and helps solve, reduce or improve the problem. Assume everything in the statement is true.

Statement: A large number of commuters were stranded after a bridge on the highway collapsed following heavy rain.

I. Traffic should immediately be diverted through alternative routes.
II. Other old bridges on the route should be inspected for safety.

- **A.** Both I and II follow ✅
- **B.** Only II follows  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Only I follows  _(error: accepted only I — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Neither I nor II follows  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: Diversion gives immediate relief — follows.
2. II: Inspection prevents a repeat on the same route — follows.
3. Hence: Both I and II follow.

**Formula:** A course of action is a practicable and feasible step which, if taken, follows logically from the statement and helps solve, reduce or improve the problem  
**Trap:** Immediate relief and prevention can both be valid.

## GRB-288 · L2 · medium · Course of action · foundation

A course of action is a practicable and feasible step which, if taken, follows logically from the statement and helps solve, reduce or improve the problem. Assume everything in the statement is true.

Statement: Stray dogs have bitten several children in a residential colony.

I. The municipal body should carry out sterilisation and vaccination of stray dogs in the area.
II. All residents should be asked to move out of the colony.

- **A.** Neither I nor II follows  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Both I and II follow  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Only II follows  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Only I follows ✅

**Working**

1. I: Addresses the problem practically — follows.
2. II: Evacuating a colony is impractical and disproportionate — does not follow.
3. Hence: Only I follows.

**Formula:** A course of action is a practicable and feasible step which, if taken, follows logically from the statement and helps solve, reduce or improve the problem  
**Trap:** Drastic, impractical steps are not valid courses of action.

## GRB-289 · L2 · medium · Course of action · foundation

A course of action is a practicable and feasible step which, if taken, follows logically from the statement and helps solve, reduce or improve the problem. Assume everything in the statement is true.

Statement: Sale of spurious medicines has been reported from a few shops in the town.

I. All medical shops in the state should be closed.
II. The drug control department should inspect the shops and act against those found guilty.

- **A.** Neither I nor II follows  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Only II follows ✅
- **C.** Only I follows  _(error: accepted only I — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Both I and II follow  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: Closing every shop in the state punishes the innocent and cuts off medicines — does not follow.
2. II: Targeted inspection and action fit the problem — follows.
3. Hence: Only II follows.

**Formula:** A course of action is a practicable and feasible step which, if taken, follows logically from the statement and helps solve, reduce or improve the problem  
**Trap:** The response must be proportionate to the scale of the problem.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## GRB-290 · L2 · medium · Course of action · foundation

A course of action is a practicable and feasible step which, if taken, follows logically from the statement and helps solve, reduce or improve the problem. Assume everything in the statement is true.

Statement: The prices of vegetables in the town rose slightly this week because of a local festival.

I. The government should ban the festival.
II. The government should immediately import vegetables.

- **A.** Only II follows  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Both I and II follow  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Only I follows  _(error: accepted only I — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Neither I nor II follows ✅

**Working**

1. I: Banning a festival for a slight, temporary price rise is disproportionate — does not follow.
2. II: Importing for a slight weekly rise is unnecessary — does not follow.
3. Hence: Neither I nor II follows.

**Formula:** A course of action is a practicable and feasible step which, if taken, follows logically from the statement and helps solve, reduce or improve the problem  
**Trap:** A minor, temporary problem needs no drastic action.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## GRB-291 · L2 · medium · Course of action · officer

A course of action is a practicable and feasible step which, if taken, follows logically from the statement and helps solve, reduce or improve the problem. Assume everything in the statement is true.

Statement: A survey shows that a large number of rural bank accounts opened under a financial-inclusion scheme have had no transactions for a year.

I. Banks should hold financial-literacy camps in these areas to explain the benefits of using the accounts.
II. All such accounts should be closed immediately.

- **A.** Only I follows ✅
- **B.** Neither I nor II follows  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Both I and II follow  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Only II follows  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: Addresses the reason for non-use — follows.
2. II: Closing accounts defeats the purpose of inclusion — does not follow.
3. Hence: Only I follows.

**Formula:** A course of action is a practicable and feasible step which, if taken, follows logically from the statement and helps solve, reduce or improve the problem  
**Trap:** The step should advance, not reverse, the objective of the scheme.

## GRB-292 · L2 · medium · Course of action · officer

A course of action is a practicable and feasible step which, if taken, follows logically from the statement and helps solve, reduce or improve the problem. Assume everything in the statement is true.

Statement: Several cases of food poisoning have been reported after a community feast in a town.

I. Samples of the food served should be tested to find the source of contamination.
II. Those affected should be given immediate medical treatment.

- **A.** Both I and II follow ✅
- **B.** Only I follows  _(error: accepted only I — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Neither I nor II follows  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Only II follows  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: Identifies the cause and prevents recurrence — follows.
2. II: Immediate relief for victims — follows.
3. Hence: Both I and II follow.

**Formula:** A course of action is a practicable and feasible step which, if taken, follows logically from the statement and helps solve, reduce or improve the problem  
**Trap:** Relief and investigation are complementary.

## GRB-293 · L3 · hard · Course of action · officer

A course of action is a practicable and feasible step which, if taken, follows logically from the statement and helps solve, reduce or improve the problem. Assume everything in the statement is true.

Statement: Water in the city's reservoirs has fallen to its lowest level in ten years, and the monsoon is still six weeks away.

I. The civic body should stop supplying water to households until the monsoon arrives.
II. The civic body should regulate the water supply and appeal to citizens to avoid wastage.

- **A.** Only I follows  _(error: accepted only I — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Neither I nor II follows  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Both I and II follow  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Only II follows ✅

**Working**

1. I: Stopping supply for six weeks is neither feasible nor humane — does not follow.
2. II: Rationing and conservation stretch the available water — follows.
3. Hence: Only II follows.

**Formula:** A course of action is a practicable and feasible step which, if taken, follows logically from the statement and helps solve, reduce or improve the problem  
**Trap:** Choose management over total shutdown.

## GRB-294 · L3 · hard · Course of action · officer

A course of action is a practicable and feasible step which, if taken, follows logically from the statement and helps solve, reduce or improve the problem. Assume everything in the statement is true.

Statement: An audit found that many government hospitals have expensive equipment lying unused because trained technicians are not available.

I. The government should recruit or train technicians to operate the equipment.
II. The unused equipment should be sold to private hospitals.

- **A.** Only I follows ✅
- **B.** Both I and II follow  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Neither I nor II follows  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Only II follows  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: Removes the cause of non-use — follows.
2. II: Selling deprives public patients of facilities already bought — does not follow.
3. Hence: Only I follows.

**Formula:** A course of action is a practicable and feasible step which, if taken, follows logically from the statement and helps solve, reduce or improve the problem  
**Trap:** Solve the bottleneck rather than give up the asset.

## GRB-295 · L3 · hard · Course of action · officer

A course of action is a practicable and feasible step which, if taken, follows logically from the statement and helps solve, reduce or improve the problem. Assume everything in the statement is true.

Statement: Many first-time investors lost money after following unregistered 'stock tip' channels on social media.

I. The market regulator should run awareness campaigns urging investors to deal only with registered advisers.
II. Action should be taken against those running unregistered advisory channels.

- **A.** Both I and II follow ✅
- **B.** Only II follows  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Only I follows  _(error: accepted only I — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Neither I nor II follows  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: Prevents future losses through awareness — follows.
2. II: Enforcement against the source — follows.
3. Hence: Both I and II follow.

**Formula:** A course of action is a practicable and feasible step which, if taken, follows logically from the statement and helps solve, reduce or improve the problem  
**Trap:** Awareness and enforcement address the problem from two sides.

A course of action is a practicable and feasible step which, if taken, follows logically from the statement and helps solve, reduce or improve the problem. Assume everything in the statement is true.

- **A.** Both I and II follow  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Neither I nor II follows  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Only I follows ✅
- **D.** Only II follows  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: Stops the pollution at source — follows.
2. II: Absurd and impossible — does not follow.
3. Hence: Only I follows.

**Formula:** A course of action is a practicable and feasible step which, if taken, follows logically from the statement and helps solve, reduce or improve the problem

## GRB-297 · L3 · hard · Course of action · officer

A course of action is a practicable and feasible step which, if taken, follows logically from the statement and helps solve, reduce or improve the problem. Assume everything in the statement is true.

Statement: Attendance at evening adult-literacy classes in a village has fallen because most learners work in the fields until late.

I. The class timings should be changed to suit the learners.
II. The adult-literacy programme in the village should be discontinued.

- **A.** Only II follows  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Only I follows ✅
- **C.** Both I and II follow  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Neither I nor II follows  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: Addresses the actual cause (timing clash) — follows.
2. II: Abandoning the programme does not solve the problem — does not follow.
3. Hence: Only I follows.

**Formula:** A course of action is a practicable and feasible step which, if taken, follows logically from the statement and helps solve, reduce or improve the problem

## GRB-298 · L3 · hard · Course of action · officer

A course of action is a practicable and feasible step which, if taken, follows logically from the statement and helps solve, reduce or improve the problem. Assume everything in the statement is true.

Statement: Some passengers complained that the air-conditioning in one coach of a train was not working on a particular day.

I. The railways should replace the air-conditioning systems in all its trains.
II. The railways should stop running air-conditioned coaches.

- **A.** Both I and II follow  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Only I follows  _(error: accepted only I — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Only II follows  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Neither I nor II follows ✅

**Working**

1. I: An isolated fault does not justify replacing the system across all trains — does not follow.
2. II: Withdrawing the service altogether is absurd — does not follow.
3. Hence: Neither I nor II follows.

**Formula:** A course of action is a practicable and feasible step which, if taken, follows logically from the statement and helps solve, reduce or improve the problem  
**Trap:** The proper action (repairing that coach) is not among the options.

A course of action is a practicable and feasible step which, if taken, follows logically from the statement and helps solve, reduce or improve the problem. Assume everything in the statement is true.

I. The road should be closed permanently.
II. An inquiry should be held into the quality of work, and the contractor should be made to repair the road.

- **A.** Neither I nor II follows  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Only II follows ✅
- **C.** Only I follows  _(error: accepted only I — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Both I and II follow  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: Permanent closure is impractical — does not follow.
2. II: Fixes accountability and the defect — follows.
3. Hence: Only II follows.

**Formula:** A course of action is a practicable and feasible step which, if taken, follows logically from the statement and helps solve, reduce or improve the problem  
**Trap:** Accountability plus repair is the proportionate response.

A course of action is a practicable and feasible step which, if taken, follows logically from the statement and helps solve, reduce or improve the problem. Assume everything in the statement is true.

I. Banks and the police should publicise official helpline numbers and warn customers against searching for such numbers online.

- **A.** Neither I nor II follows  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Both I and II follow ✅
- **C.** Only I follows  _(error: accepted only I — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Only II follows  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

2. II: Faster response and enforcement — follows.

## GRB-301 · L1 · easy · Statement and argument — strong or weak · foundation

A strong argument is both important and directly related to the question. A weak argument is of minor importance, not directly related to the question, or rests on a trivial or false generalisation.

Should mobile phones be banned in examination halls?

I. Yes. They can be used to cheat during the examination.
II. No. Students should be free to carry whatever they like.

- **A.** Both I and II are strong  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Only argument I is strong ✅
- **C.** Neither I nor II is strong  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Only argument II is strong  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: Directly relevant and important — strong.
2. II: Rests on an unreasonable claim of absolute freedom — weak.
3. Hence: Only argument I is strong.

**Formula:** A strong argument is both important and directly related to the question  
**Trap:** An argument based on unlimited freedom is weak.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## GRB-302 · L2 · medium · Statement and argument — strong or weak · foundation

A strong argument is both important and directly related to the question. A weak argument is of minor importance, not directly related to the question, or rests on a trivial or false generalisation.

Should the retirement age of government employees be reduced to 55 years?

I. Yes. It will create more openings for younger job-seekers.
II. No. The government will lose experienced employees prematurely, affecting efficiency.

- **A.** Only argument I is strong  _(error: accepted only I — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Both I and II are strong ✅
- **C.** Neither I nor II is strong  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Only argument II is strong  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: Important and relevant — strong.
2. II: Important and relevant — strong.
3. Hence: Both I and II are strong.

**Formula:** A strong argument is both important and directly related to the question  
**Trap:** Both sides can be strong at once.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## GRB-303 · L1 · easy · Statement and argument — strong or weak · foundation

A strong argument is both important and directly related to the question. A weak argument is of minor importance, not directly related to the question, or rests on a trivial or false generalisation.

Should cycling be encouraged in cities?

I. No. Cycles are cheaper than cars.
II. Yes. Cycling reduces pollution and improves health.

- **A.** Both I and II are strong  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Neither I nor II is strong  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Only argument I is strong  _(error: accepted only I — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Only argument II is strong ✅

**Working**

1. I: Being cheaper is not a reason against encouraging cycling — weak.
2. II: Relevant and important — strong.
3. Hence: Only argument II is strong.

**Formula:** A strong argument is both important and directly related to the question  
**Trap:** Check that the reason actually supports the side it claims.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## GRB-304 · L2 · medium · Statement and argument — strong or weak · foundation

A strong argument is both important and directly related to the question. A weak argument is of minor importance, not directly related to the question, or rests on a trivial or false generalisation.

Should every school have a playground?

I. Yes. Many famous players studied in schools.
II. No. Playgrounds make a lot of noise.

- **A.** Neither I nor II is strong ✅
- **B.** Both I and II are strong  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Only argument I is strong  _(error: accepted only I — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Only argument II is strong  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: Irrelevant link — weak.
2. II: Trivial objection — weak.
3. Hence: Neither I nor II is strong.

**Formula:** A strong argument is both important and directly related to the question  
**Trap:** Trivial or irrelevant reasons make weak arguments.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## GRB-305 · L1 · easy · Statement and argument — strong or weak · foundation

A strong argument is both important and directly related to the question. A weak argument is of minor importance, not directly related to the question, or rests on a trivial or false generalisation.

II. No. Shopkeepers will have to print new bills.

- **B.** Both I and II are strong  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Only argument I is strong ✅
- **D.** Neither I nor II is strong  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: Relevant and important — strong.
2. II: Irrelevant/trivial — weak.
3. Hence: Only argument I is strong.

**Formula:** A strong argument is both important and directly related to the question  
**Trap:** A trivial inconvenience is a weak counter-argument.

- **D.** Only argument I is strong  _(error: accepted only I — misjudged one of the two)_

- **D.** Both I and II are strong  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_

1. I: Imitation of other countries is not a reason by itself — weak.

## GRB-316 · L1 · easy · Statement and inference · foundation

Consider the statement(s) to be true even if they seem at variance with commonly known facts. An inference follows only if it can be definitely concluded from the statement(s).

Statements: All the students of Class X passed the examination. Ravi is a student of Class X.

I. Ravi passed the examination.
II. Ravi scored the highest marks in Class X.

- **A.** Neither I nor II follows  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Only II follows  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Only I follows ✅
- **D.** Both I and II follow  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: Follows directly from 'all passed'.
2. II: Nothing is said about ranks.
3. Hence: Only I follows.

**Formula:** Consider the statement(s) to be true even if they seem at variance with commonly known facts  
**Trap:** Stay within what is stated.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## GRB-317 · L1 · easy · Statement and inference · foundation

Consider the statement(s) to be true even if they seem at variance with commonly known facts. An inference follows only if it can be definitely concluded from the statement(s).

Statement: The shop opens at 9 a.m. and closes at 8 p.m. on all days except Sunday, when it stays closed.

I. The shop is open for eleven hours on Monday.
II. The shop is closed on Sunday.

- **A.** Only I follows  _(error: accepted only I — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Only II follows  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Both I and II follow ✅
- **D.** Neither I nor II follows  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: 9 a.m. to 8 p.m. = 11 hours — follows.
2. II: Stated directly — follows.
3. Hence: Both I and II follow.

**Formula:** Consider the statement(s) to be true even if they seem at variance with commonly known facts  
**Trap:** Compute where the data allow.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## GRB-318 · L1 · easy · Statement and inference · foundation

Consider the statement(s) to be true even if they seem at variance with commonly known facts. An inference follows only if it can be definitely concluded from the statement(s).

Statement: Most of the members of the club are doctors.

I. Some members of the club are doctors.
II. All doctors in the city are members of the club.

- **A.** Neither I nor II follows  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Both I and II follow  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Only I follows ✅
- **D.** Only II follows  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: 'Most' includes 'some' — follows.
2. II: The statement says nothing about all doctors — does not follow.
3. Hence: Only I follows.

**Formula:** Consider the statement(s) to be true even if they seem at variance with commonly known facts  
**Trap:** Do not reverse the direction of a statement.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## GRB-319 · L2 · medium · Statement and inference · foundation

Consider the statement(s) to be true even if they seem at variance with commonly known facts. An inference follows only if it can be definitely concluded from the statement(s).

Statement: Sales of woollen clothes in the town rose sharply in December.

I. Every family in the town bought woollen clothes in December.
II. Woollen clothes are available only in December.

- **A.** Only I follows  _(error: accepted only I — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Only II follows  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Both I and II follow  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Neither I nor II follows ✅

**Working**

1. I: A sharp rise does not mean every family bought — does not follow.
2. II: Nothing about availability — does not follow.
3. Hence: Neither I nor II follows.

**Formula:** Consider the statement(s) to be true even if they seem at variance with commonly known facts  
**Trap:** Reject 'every' and 'only' unless the statement supports them.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## GRB-320 · L1 · easy · Statement and inference · foundation

Consider the statement(s) to be true even if they seem at variance with commonly known facts. An inference follows only if it can be definitely concluded from the statement(s).

Statements: No bird in the zoo is a parrot. Tweety is a bird in the zoo.

I. Tweety is not a parrot.
II. There are no parrots anywhere.

- **A.** Neither I nor II follows  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Both I and II follow  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Only II follows  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Only I follows ✅

**Working**

1. I: Follows from the two statements.
2. II: The statement is about the zoo only — does not follow.
3. Hence: Only I follows.

**Formula:** Consider the statement(s) to be true even if they seem at variance with commonly known facts  
**Trap:** Mind the scope of the statement.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## GRB-321 · L3 · hard · Statement and inference · officer

Consider the statement(s) to be true even if they seem at variance with commonly known facts. An inference follows only if it can be definitely concluded from the statement(s).

Statement: The company's profit this year was ₹50 crore, double that of last year, although its revenue rose by only 10%.

I. The company's profit last year was ₹25 crore.
II. This year the company's costs grew by less than 10%.

- **A.** Only II follows  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Both I and II follow ✅
- **C.** Only I follows  _(error: accepted only I — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Neither I nor II follows  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: Double of last year = 50 → last year 25 — follows.
2. II: Let last year's revenue be R: costs went from R − 25 to 1.1R − 50, and 1.1R − 50 < 1.1(R − 25); so costs grew by less than 10% — follows.
3. Hence: Both I and II follow.

**Formula:** Consider the statement(s) to be true even if they seem at variance with commonly known facts  
**Trap:** Profit can double on small revenue growth only if costs grew more slowly than revenue.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## GRB-322 · L2 · medium · Statement and inference · officer

Consider the statement(s) to be true even if they seem at variance with commonly known facts. An inference follows only if it can be definitely concluded from the statement(s).

Statement: Admission records show that 400 ticket-holders entered the museum on Saturday. Children below 12 enter free and need no ticket.

I. At least 400 people visited the museum on Saturday.
II. No child below 12 visited the museum on Saturday.

- **A.** Neither I nor II follows  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Only II follows  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Only I follows ✅
- **D.** Both I and II follow  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: 400 ticket-holders entered — so at least 400 visitors — follows.
2. II: Children need no ticket, so they would not appear in the count — does not follow.
3. Hence: Only I follows.

**Formula:** Consider the statement(s) to be true even if they seem at variance with commonly known facts  
**Trap:** A count of ticket-holders says nothing about those who need no ticket.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## GRB-323 · L2 · medium · Statement and inference · officer

Consider the statement(s) to be true even if they seem at variance with commonly known facts. An inference follows only if it can be definitely concluded from the statement(s).

Statement: The price of milk rose from ₹50 to ₹55 per litre, while a family's monthly milk consumption stayed at 30 litres.

I. The family's monthly spending on milk rose by ₹150.
II. The family's total monthly spending rose by 10%.

- **A.** Only II follows  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Only I follows ✅
- **C.** Both I and II follow  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Neither I nor II follows  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: 30 × ₹5 = ₹150 — follows.
2. II: Only milk spending rose 10%; total spending is unknown — does not follow.
3. Hence: Only I follows.

**Formula:** Consider the statement(s) to be true even if they seem at variance with commonly known facts  
**Trap:** A 10% rise in one item is not a 10% rise in the total.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## GRB-324 · L2 · medium · Statement and inference · officer

Consider the statement(s) to be true even if they seem at variance with commonly known facts. An inference follows only if it can be definitely concluded from the statement(s).

Statements: Some managers are engineers. All engineers are graduates.

I. All graduates are engineers.
II. Some managers are graduates.

- **A.** Only II follows ✅
- **B.** Both I and II follow  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Neither I nor II follows  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Only I follows  _(error: accepted only I — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: 'All engineers are graduates' cannot be reversed — does not follow.
2. II: The managers who are engineers are graduates — follows.
3. Hence: Only II follows.

**Formula:** Consider the statement(s) to be true even if they seem at variance with commonly known facts  
**Trap:** An 'all' statement does not convert to 'all' in reverse.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## GRB-325 · L3 · hard · Statement and inference · officer

Consider the statement(s) to be true even if they seem at variance with commonly known facts. An inference follows only if it can be definitely concluded from the statement(s).

Statement: A train left its origin on time but reached its destination 45 minutes late. The scheduled journey time is 5 hours.

I. The train took 5 hours 45 minutes for the journey.
II. The train's average speed over the journey was lower than scheduled.

- **A.** Both I and II follow ✅
- **B.** Only II follows  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Neither I nor II follows  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Only I follows  _(error: accepted only I — misjudged one of the two)_

**Working**

1. I: Left on time, 45 minutes late → 5 h 45 min — follows.
2. II: Same distance in more time → lower average speed — follows.
3. Hence: Both I and II follow.

**Formula:** Consider the statement(s) to be true even if they seem at variance with commonly known facts  
**Trap:** Average speed = distance ÷ time.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## GRB-326 · L3 · hard · Statement and inference · officer

Consider the statement(s) to be true even if they seem at variance with commonly known facts. An inference follows only if it can be definitely concluded from the statement(s).

Statement: No official of the bank who handles cash is allowed to approve loans. Kiran, an official of the bank, approves loans.

I. Kiran does not handle cash.
II. Kiran is not an official of the bank.

- **A.** Neither I nor II follows  _(error: rejected both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **B.** Only II follows  _(error: accepted only II — misjudged one of the two)_
- **C.** Both I and II follow  _(error: accepted both — misjudged one of the two)_
- **D.** Only I follows ✅

**Working**

1. I: Kiran is an official who approves loans, so she cannot be one who handles cash — follows.
2. II: Contradicts the statement — does not follow.
3. Hence: Only I follows.

**Formula:** Consider the statement(s) to be true even if they seem at variance with commonly known facts  
**Trap:** Use the rule on the member described.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## GRB-327 · L2 · medium · Statement and inference · officer

Consider the statement(s) to be true even if they seem at variance with commonly known facts. An inference follows only if it can be definitely concluded from the statement(s).

Statements: Every member of the audit team is a chartered accountant. Some chartered accountants in the firm are also cost accountants. Meena is a member of the audit team.

Which of the following definitely follows?

- **A.** Some members of the audit team are cost accountants.  _(error: the cost-accountant CAs may be outside the team)_
- **B.** Meena is also a cost accountant.  _(error: 'some' CAs being cost accountants says nothing about Meena)_
- **C.** Meena is a chartered accountant. ✅
- **D.** Every cost accountant in the firm is on the audit team.  _(error: reversal / overgeneralisation)_

**Working**

1. Meena ∈ audit team ⊂ chartered accountants → Meena is a CA.
2. The other options need facts not given.

**Formula:** Set inclusion  
**Trap:** Only chains of 'all' statements give certain conclusions.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## GRB-328 · L3 · hard · Statement and inference · officer

Consider the statement(s) to be true even if they seem at variance with commonly known facts. An inference follows only if it can be definitely concluded from the statement(s).

Statements: In a town, every school that has a library also has a computer laboratory. School P has no computer laboratory.

Which of the following definitely follows?

- **A.** School P does not have a library. ✅
- **B.** Every school with a laboratory also has a library.  _(error: converse of the rule)_
- **C.** School P has a library but no laboratory.  _(error: contradicts the rule)_
- **D.** School P will soon set up a laboratory.  _(error: speculation)_

**Working**

1. Library → laboratory. No laboratory → no library (contrapositive).

**Formula:** If A then B ⇒ if not B then not A  
**Trap:** The converse (B → A) does not follow.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## GRB-329 · L3 · hard · Statement and inference · officer

Consider the statement(s) to be true even if they seem at variance with commonly known facts. An inference follows only if it can be definitely concluded from the statement(s).

Statement: In an office of 60 employees, 35 speak Hindi and 30 speak English. Every employee speaks at least one of the two languages.

Which of the following definitely follows?

- **A.** 35 employees speak only Hindi.  _(error: forgot to subtract those who speak both)_
- **B.** More employees speak only English than only Hindi.  _(error: only English = 25, only Hindi = 30)_
- **C.** Exactly 5 employees speak both languages. ✅
- **D.** No employee speaks both languages.  _(error: 35 + 30 exceeds 60)_

**Working**

1. Both = 35 + 30 − 60 = 5.
2. Only Hindi = 30, only English = 25.

**Formula:** n(A ∪ B) = n(A) + n(B) − n(A ∩ B)  
**Trap:** Everyone speaks at least one language, so the union is the whole office.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## GRB-330 · L3 · hard · Statement and inference · officer

Consider the statement(s) to be true even if they seem at variance with commonly known facts. An inference follows only if it can be definitely concluded from the statement(s).

Statement: After a new metro line opened, the number of buses on Route 12 was cut from 40 to 25. The metro now carries 60,000 passengers a day along the same corridor.

Which of the following can definitely be inferred?

- **A.** All former bus passengers on Route 12 now use the metro.  _(error: not stated)_
- **B.** The metro line has reduced air pollution in the corridor.  _(error: not stated)_
- **C.** The number of buses on Route 12 fell by 37.5%. ✅
- **D.** Daily bus passengers on Route 12 fell by 60,000.  _(error: metro riders need not all be former bus users)_

**Working**

1. (40 − 25) ÷ 40 = 37.5%.
2. Nothing is stated about where metro passengers came from.

**Formula:** Percentage change = change ÷ original  
**Trap:** Only computations from given numbers are certain.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## GRB-331 · L1 · easy · Cause and effect · foundation

Read the two statements. They may be related as cause and effect, may both be effects of a common cause, or may be effects of unrelated causes. Choose the relationship that fits best.

I. Heavy rain lashed the city throughout the night.
II. Several low-lying areas of the city were waterlogged in the morning.

- **A.** Statement II is the cause and statement I is its effect  _(error: took II as the cause)_
- **B.** Both the statements are effects of independent causes  _(error: treated them as unrelated effects)_
- **C.** Statement I is the cause and statement II is its effect ✅
- **D.** Both the statements are effects of some common cause  _(error: looked for a common cause)_

**Working**

1. Night-long rain directly leads to waterlogging.
2. Hence: Statement I is the cause and statement II is its effect.

**Formula:** Cause precedes and explains effect  
**Trap:** An event reported later in the text can still be the cause.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## GRB-346 · L2 · medium · Strengthening and weakening an argument · foundation

Argument: Students who eat breakfast regularly score higher in morning tests. So eating breakfast improves concentration.

Which of the following, if true, most weakens the argument?

- **A.** Students who eat breakfast also sleep longer, and longer sleep is known to raise test scores. ✅
- **B.** Some students prefer to drink tea rather than milk with their breakfast on school days.  _(error: irrelevant detail)_
- **C.** Morning tests in the school are held at 9 a.m. on every working day.  _(error: irrelevant detail)_
- **D.** Breakfast is usually the first meal that students eat after waking up in the morning.  _(error: irrelevant restatement)_

**Working**

1. The weakener offers an alternative cause (sleep) for the higher scores.

**Formula:** Alternative cause weakens a causal claim  
**Trap:** Irrelevant details neither strengthen nor weaken.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## GRB-347 · L2 · medium · Strengthening and weakening an argument · foundation

Argument: Town X installed street lights on its main roads last year, and night-time thefts on those roads fell by 40%. So street lights reduce theft.

Which of the following, if true, most strengthens the argument?

- **A.** Town X has a population of about two lakh people spread over ten wards.  _(error: irrelevant)_
- **B.** Night-time thefts on the unlit roads of the same town did not fall during that period. ✅
- **C.** Daytime thefts on the main roads rose slightly during the same period as the lighting work.  _(error: does not bear on night-time effect of lights)_
- **D.** The new street lights were purchased last year from a supplier based in the same town.  _(error: irrelevant)_

**Working**

1. A comparison group without lights showing no fall supports the lights as the cause.

**Formula:** Control comparison strengthens a causal claim  
**Trap:** Look for evidence that rules out other explanations.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## GRB-348 · L2 · medium · Strengthening and weakening an argument · foundation

Argument: The company should move its office to the suburbs, because rents there are 30% lower.

Which of the following, if true, most weakens the argument?

- **A.** The company was founded ten years ago by two engineers from the city.  _(error: irrelevant)_
- **B.** Most employees would face two extra hours of commuting, and many have said they would resign. ✅
- **C.** Rents in the city centre have remained almost unchanged for the last two financial years.  _(error: does not remove the rent saving)_
- **D.** The suburbs have more parks, open spaces and quieter streets than the city centre has.  _(error: irrelevant or mildly supportive)_

**Working**

1. Losing staff can outweigh the rent saving.

**Formula:** A serious cost of the plan weakens it  
**Trap:** A weakener must bear on the conclusion.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## GRB-349 · L2 · medium · Strengthening and weakening an argument · foundation

Argument: Reading a newspaper daily improves general awareness, so aspirants who read a newspaper daily do better in the general-awareness section.

Which of the following, if true, most strengthens the argument?

- **A.** Some newspapers cost more than others because they carry more pages and supplements.  _(error: irrelevant)_
- **B.** Newspapers are printed late at night and delivered early in the morning.  _(error: irrelevant)_
- **C.** Many aspirants now read the news on their phones instead of buying a printed newspaper.  _(error: does not show better performance)_
- **D.** Among 2,000 similar aspirants, daily newspaper readers scored far higher in general awareness. ✅

**Working**

1. Comparative evidence with similar backgrounds supports the link.

**Formula:** Supporting evidence with controls  
**Trap:** Choose evidence that links the cause to the outcome.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## GRB-350 · L2 · medium · Strengthening and weakening an argument · foundation

Argument: The new medicine cures this type of fever, because all ten patients who took it recovered within three days.

Which of the following, if true, most weakens the argument?

- **A.** The medicine is manufactured by a company based in India.  _(error: irrelevant)_
- **B.** The medicine is given as a tablet twice a day, after meals, for three days.  _(error: irrelevant)_
- **C.** All ten patients in the trial were adults aged between 20 and 50 years.  _(error: limits scope slightly but does not undercut the cure claim)_
- **D.** Such fevers normally subside within three days even without any medicine. ✅

**Working**

1. If recovery happens anyway, the medicine's effect is not shown.

**Formula:** Natural recovery weakens a cure claim  
**Trap:** Ask what would have happened without the cause.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## GRB-351 · L3 · hard · Strengthening and weakening an argument · officer

Argument: Within six months of the city making bus travel free for women, women's use of public transport rose by 25%. Fare-free travel is therefore the key to getting more women onto public transport.

Which of the following, if true, most weakens the argument?

- **A.** The scheme costs the city a substantial amount of money every month.  _(error: a cost, not evidence against the effect)_
- **B.** Women make up roughly half of the city's population, according to the latest census data.  _(error: irrelevant)_
- **C.** Some women were already travelling on monthly passes before the scheme began last year.  _(error: does not explain the rise)_
- **D.** The city also added 300 buses in that period, and men's ridership rose by a similar 25%. ✅

**Working**

1. An equal rise among men (who pay) points to better service, not free fares, as the driver.

**Formula:** Rival explanation with comparison group  
**Trap:** Check whether the change is specific to the group affected.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## GRB-352 · L3 · hard · Strengthening and weakening an argument · officer

Argument: Since the bank began offering loans through its mobile app, its loan disbursals have risen by 40%. The app is therefore responsible for the growth.

Which of the following, if true, most strengthens the argument?

- **A.** Interest rates on loans fell across the banking industry during the same period.  _(error: rival explanation — weakens)_
- **B.** Similar banks without such apps saw flat disbursals, and 90% of new loans came via the app. ✅
- **C.** The bank also trained all its branch staff in selling loan products during the same year.  _(error: rival explanation — weakens)_
- **D.** The app was designed and is maintained by an outside technology firm based in Bengaluru.  _(error: irrelevant)_

**Working**

1. A flat comparison group and the channel data both tie the growth to the app.

**Formula:** Eliminate rival explanations  
**Trap:** Industry-wide changes weaken a claim about one cause.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## GRB-353 · L3 · hard · Strengthening and weakening an argument · officer

Argument: Company A should acquire Company B, because B's sales grew by 60% last year.

Which of the following, if true, most weakens the argument?

- **A.** B's growth came from a one-time government order, and its regular sales are falling. ✅
- **B.** B's sales had also grown steadily in each of the three previous years.  _(error: strengthens)_
- **C.** B is located in a different state from the one that houses A's headquarters and factories.  _(error: minor)_
- **D.** A's chief executive officer worked at B as a senior manager several years ago.  _(error: irrelevant)_

**Working**

1. If the growth will not continue, it is a poor basis for acquisition.

**Formula:** Unrepresentative data weaken a conclusion  
**Trap:** Ask whether past performance will continue.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## GRB-354 · L3 · hard · Strengthening and weakening an argument · officer

Argument: Cities with more gyms have lower rates of heart disease, so building more gyms will reduce heart disease.

Which of the following, if true, most weakens the argument?

- **A.** Some of the gyms in these cities also have swimming pools and saunas.  _(error: irrelevant)_
- **B.** Heart disease is one of the leading causes of death in many countries around the world.  _(error: irrelevant background)_
- **C.** Most gyms in these cities charge a fixed monthly membership fee along with an entry fee.  _(error: irrelevant)_
- **D.** Gyms tend to open in richer cities, where people also eat better and get better healthcare. ✅

**Working**

1. A third factor (wealth) may explain both gyms and lower disease.

**Formula:** Correlation vs causation (confounder)  
**Trap:** A common cause undermines a causal inference from correlation.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## GRB-355 · L3 · hard · Strengthening and weakening an argument · officer

Argument: Introducing a congestion charge in the central business district will reduce traffic jams there.

Which of the following, if true, most strengthens the argument?

- **A.** The central business district contains a large number of offices.  _(error: irrelevant background)_
- **B.** Many motorists say that they dislike paying any charge at all to enter the district.  _(error: dislike does not show reduced traffic)_
- **C.** In a comparable city, a similar charge cut peak-hour traffic in its zone by about 20%. ✅
- **D.** The charge will be collected electronically through cameras installed at all entry points.  _(error: implementation detail)_

**Working**

1. Evidence from a comparable case supports the predicted effect.

**Formula:** Analogy with a comparable case  
**Trap:** Evidence of effect beats statements of attitude.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## GRB-356 · L3 · hard · Strengthening and weakening an argument · officer

Plan: A state plans to raise farm incomes by distributing high-yield seeds free of cost.

Which of the following, if true, casts the most doubt on the plan's success?

- **A.** The state is divided into thirty districts and over two hundred blocks for administration.  _(error: irrelevant)_
- **B.** The high-yield seeds need assured irrigation, which only 20% of the state's farmland has. ✅
- **C.** The seeds will be packed in bags printed with sowing instructions in the local language.  _(error: irrelevant/supportive)_
- **D.** Farmers in the state have generally welcomed free inputs supplied by the government.  _(error: supportive)_

**Working**

1. If most farms cannot use the seeds, incomes will not rise much.

**Formula:** Missing precondition undermines a plan  
**Trap:** Look for a condition the plan depends on.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:
